This environment is used exclusively for engine verification, not as a game level. 
 
9.3 Player Control Validation 
Checklist for player behavior: 
1.​ Movement​
 
○​ Smooth left/right movement​
 
○​ Correct acceleration and friction​
 
○​ Proper handling on slopes​
 
○​ No unintended sliding or jittering​
 
2.​ Jumping​
 
○​ Single jump works reliably​
 
○​ Jump only allowed when grounded (no infinite jumps)​
 
○​ Proper transition Jump → Fall → Land​
 
3.​ Collision​
 
○​ No tunneling through ground​
 

 
○​ Correct interaction with walls and ceilings​
 
○​ No getting stuck in corners​
 
4.​ Input Mapping​
 
○​ All core actions (move, jump, attack, interact) respond correctly​
 
○​ Input can be disabled (e.g., via a debug toggle) and re-enabled​
 
 
9.4 FSM Behavior Validation 
Checklist for FSM: 
●​ States change as expected:​
 
○​ Idle ↔ Run​
 
○​ Run → Jump → Fall → Land → Idle​
 
○​ Any → Hurt → Idle​
 
○​ Any → Death​
 
●​ No orphan states (states that cannot be reached or exited)​
 
●​ Transitions log correctly when debug FSM is enabled​
 
●​ Animation names match state mappings​
 
Any misalignment between state and animation should be visible in debug output. 
 
9.5 Stats & Damage Validation 
Checklist for StatsComponent: 
●​ HP and MP initialize to correct values​
 
●​ Damage reduces HP as per formula​
 

 
●​ HP never exceeds Max HP​
 
●​ HP reaching 0 triggers Death state exactly once​
 
●​ XP rewards from killing enemies are accumulated correctly (even if not yet spent at 
rest points)​
 
Runtime vs base stats must remain distinct. 
 
9.6 Combat Loop Validation 
Checklist for CombatComponent and hit/hurtboxes: 
●​ Player attacks correctly spawn active hitboxes​
 
●​ Hitboxes only deal damage when active​
 
●​ Enemies take damage when hit and show Hurt state​
 
●​ Knockback is applied and respects collisions​
 
●​ Invulnerability frames prevent repeated instant hits​
 
●​ Death disables combat and hitboxes for the dead actor​
 
Debug overlay should show: 
●​ Which hitboxes are active​
 
●​ Damage values​
 
●​ Kill events​
 
 
9.7 Enemy AI Behavior Validation 
Checklist for EnemyAIController: 
●​ Idle → Patrol transitions work as expected​
 
●​ Patrol routes behave correctly (boundaries or waypoints)​
 

 
●​ Enemies detect player within detection area​
 
●​ Enemies chase player and respect terrain​
 
●​ Enemies attack when in range​
 
●​ Enemies return to patrol/idle when player leaves detection area​
 
●​ Enemies enter Death state when HP = 0 and are cleaned up afterward​
 
Debug overlay should show current AI mode and detection status. 
 
9.8 Animation Integration Validation 
Checklist for AnimationComponent: 
●​ Animations correctly play per FSM state (Idle, Run, Jump, Fall, Land, Attack, Hurt, 
Death)​
 
●​ Attack animations fire frame tags at correct timing for hitbox activation​
 
●​ Death animation completes before cleanup​
 
●​ Facing direction (sprite flip) matches movement / target direction​
 
Any missing or incorrect animation mapping should be clearly logged. 
 
9.9 Debug & Tooling Validation 
Checklist for DebugManager and overlays: 
●​ Global debug toggle works (on/off)​
 
●​ FSM debug shows correct state names​
 
●​ AI debug shows correct modes​
 
●​ Combat debug correctly logs hits and deaths​
 
●​ Collision/hitbox visualization is legible and aligned with shapes​
 

 
Debug must be non-intrusive when disabled and efficient enough for normal use when 
enabled. 
 
9.10 Regression Check Against Phase Zero 
Ensure that Phase One changes did not break: 
●​ Basic movement stability​
 
●​ Collision response from Phase Zero​
 
●​ TileMap collision behavior​
 
If any regression appears, fix collision/movement first before layering more logic. 
 
9.11 Success Criteria for Phase One Completion 
Phase One is considered complete when: 
1.​ Player and at least two enemy types fully function in the test scene:​
 
○​ Move​
 
○​ Attack​
 
○​ Take damage​
 
○​ Die​
 
2.​ FSM, Stats, Combat, AI, Animation, Input, and Debug systems work together without 
fatal errors.​
 
3.​ No major regressions remain from Phase Zero collision behavior.​
 
4.​ The test scene can run continuously for an extended period without errors or severe 
performance issues.​
 
 
9.12 Summary 

 
The Integration Tests & Validation Pass formalizes how Phase One is verified as stable and 
ready for expansion.​
 Once all criteria are met, the engine has a solid, battle-tested foundation for: 
●​ Advanced combat systems​
 
●​ Complex AI​
 
●​ Gadgets & items​
 
●​ Quests and narrative events​
 
●​ Temporal anomaly mechanics​
 
Phase Two will build directly on this validated core without revisiting foundational 
architecture. 
 

 
Timelines in Gameplay 

 
 

 
1. What the Diagram Is (High-Level) 
The diagram describes Densetsu’s narrative timeline system, showing how: 

 
●​ the story unfolds through inevitable events 
●​ player agency is intentionally limited at first 
●​ agency gradually expands, but never into full control over time 
●​ choices do not change the past, but parameterize outcomes 
●​ the story converges toward three major ending threads 
●​ the tone and moral framing are embedded directly into the structure 
This is not a branching tree in the classic sense.​
 It is a guided convergence system with controlled divergence. 
 
2. Timeline Flow – Step by Step 
A. Starting Timeline 
“Today is a consequence of yesterday” 
●​ The player is thrown into the world with minimal exposition 
●​ They are not the hero, not chosen, not in control 
●​ The world already moves without them 
●​ The player adapts, bonds, and slowly feels familiar with the setting 
Tone established: 
●​ Powerlessness 
●​ Familiarization 
●​ Bonding 
●​ Motivation 
This phase communicates: 
One person alone can change very little. 
 
B. First Inevitable Crisis – The Great Fire of Hirogawa 
●​ The crisis will happen​
 
●​ The player has no agency over:​
 
○​ trigger conditions 
○​ timing 
○​ outcome 
After the crisis: 

 
●​ The player is briefly shown a glimpse of the “Perfect Future”​
 
○​ peace 
○​ prosperity 
○​ what could be, if the “right” choices are made 
Then: 
●​ The player is forced back into the “normal” timeline 
●​ All consequences and parallel events persist 
●​ Nothing is undone 
This reinforces: 
You can see a better future, but you are not told how to reach it. 
 
C. Discovery Timeline 
“The Perfect Future is a collection of past choices” 
●​ Player now understands that:​
 
○​ small choices matter 
○​ consequences may be delayed​
 
●​ However:​
 
○​ the main path still drives events 
○​ agency exists, but within rails 
This is partial agency, not freedom. 
 
D. Moral Pivot: Executioner or Liberator? 
During boss encounters (notably with 9th Wave members): 
The player may: 
●​ kill the enemy 
●​ attempt dialogue (directly or conditionally) 
●​ spare them (before or after combat, with prerequisites) 
Key rule: 
The choice does not stop events from unfolding​
 It changes how they unfold 

 
Choices can: 
●​ slightly branch the timeline 
●​ or push the story toward another outcome thread entirely 
This is where moral identity forms. 
 
E. Limited Agency Timeline 
“Accept what you cannot change, change what you cannot accept” 
The player: 
●​ begins to realize that small choices accumulate 
●​ navigates between three large outcome threads 
●​ still cannot undo major events 
●​ but can influence:​
 
○​ who survives 
○​ who defects 
○​ who assists later 
This is the philosophical core: 
You cannot control time — but you are responsible for what you do within it. 
 
F. Second Inevitable Crisis – The Western Continent / Kuro 
●​ When pursuing Okashira westward:​
 
○​ Kuro ambushes the ship 
○​ a boat crash at sea occurs 
Again: 
●​ No agency over the event 
●​ Player actions only affect parameters, not occurrence​
 
Assuming Michio’s death: 
●​ the 9th Wave loosens its grip 
●​ player actions echo forward until they become loud enough 
 

 
G. Full Agency Timeline 
“Whether you use it or not, time won’t stop for you” 
At this stage: 
●​ multiple threads are active 
●​ past choices determine which threads are accessible 
●​ the player is no longer guided — only constrained by consequences 
 
3. The Three Outcome Threads (End States) 
Thread A – GOOD 
●​ Player spared and/or incited 9th Wave generals to rebel 
●​ They assist in defeating Okashira 
●​ Good ending:​
 
○​ Okashira repents and is exiled 
○​ Major anomalies are resolved 
○​ Timeline extends into a hopeful future​
 
 
Thread B – NEUTRAL 
●​ Player had limited or no assistance 
●​ Okashira commits Harakiri upon defeat 
●​ Major anomalies remain unresolved 
●​ Bittersweet, incomplete closure 
 
Thread C – BAD 
●​ Player killed or alienated defectable generals 
●​ No assistance 
●​ Major anomalies persist 
●​ Okashira may be killed or forced to take his own life 
●​ The world remains damaged 
 
4. Structural Intent (Why This Matters) 

 
This system achieves several goals simultaneously: 
●​ ❌ avoids classic “save/load abuse” time travel 
●​ ❌ avoids false player omnipotence​
 
●​ ✅ preserves narrative cohesion 
●​ ✅ reinforces moral responsibility 
●​ ✅ makes inaction as meaningful as action 
●​ ✅ allows replayability without branching chaos 
●​ ✅ aligns mechanics with theme (“time should be left alone”)​
 
The red star at the center visually represents: 
●​ convergence 
●​ inevitability 
●​ pressure 
●​ the point where timelines feel like they explode — but actually realign​
 
 

 
Temporal Laws 

 
Temporal Laws — Densetsu 
This document defines the immutable rules governing time, causality, and 
temporal interaction in the Densetsu universe.​
It exists to prevent mechanical drift, narrative contradictions, and design 
decisions that undermine the core philosophy of the project. 
 
1. Core Principle 
Time is not a tool.​
Time exists independently of the player and cannot be owned, controlled, or rewritten. 
The act of attempting to manipulate time is framed in-universe as: 
●​ denial of reality 
●​ moral failure 
●​ a source of madness and decay 
The player may observe time, learn from it, and respond to its consequences — but never 
command it. 
 
2. Major Roles of Time in Densetsu 
2.1 Time as Continuity 
Time in Densetsu is continuous and persistent. 
●​ Major events will occur regardless of player action 
●​ The world progresses even without player involvement 
●​ Failure, delay, and inaction are valid timeline states 
[Placeholder: define how world state persistence is communicated to the player] 
 
2.2 Time as Consequence 
All player actions exist within time and generate consequences. 
●​ Choices do not branch the timeline freely 
●​ Choices parameterize how events unfold 

 
●​ Consequences may be delayed, indirect, or subtle 
Time remembers outcomes, not intentions. 
[Placeholder: link to Choice & Consequence subsystem] 
 
2.3 Time as Knowledge 
Time may be observed but not altered. 
Time Rifts 
Time Rifts are cracks in reality, not conventional anomalies. 
They function as: 
●​ unwritten history books, revealing past events beyond current knowledge 
●​ limited crystal balls, showing possible future unfoldings 
Time Rifts: 
●​ provide context, not solutions 
●​ offer hints, not instructions 
●​ increase understanding, not agency 
They exist where reality has been stressed by unresolved events, anomalies, or moral 
pressure. 
[Placeholder: presentation rules for Time Rifts (visuals, limits, frequency)] 
 
2.4 Time as Burden 
Knowledge of possible futures is not a reward. 
●​ Seeing a "Perfect Future" creates moral pressure 
●​ The player is not told how to achieve it 
●​ Responsibility increases without granting control 
Time reveals what could be, without guaranteeing that it will be. 
 
3. Inevitable Events 
Certain events are fixed points in the timeline. 

 
●​ Their triggers and outcomes cannot be prevented 
●​ Player agency begins only after their occurrence 
Examples: 
●​ The Great Fire of Hirogawa 
●​ The Western Sea Disaster 
These events exist to reinforce that the world does not revolve around the player. 
[Placeholder: catalog of fixed-point events] 
 
4. Player Agency Over Time 
4.1 What the Player Can Do 
●​ Observe past and possible future states 
●​ Influence parameters of unfolding events 
●​ Affect who survives, assists, or resists later 
●​ Accumulate moral and relational weight 
 
4.2 What the Player Can Never Do 
●​ Prevent inevitable events 
●​ Rewind or replay time 
●​ Undo consequences 
●​ Optimize outcomes through repetition 
●​ Treat time as a puzzle or resource 
These restrictions are narrative laws, not balance decisions. 
 
5. Conditional Exceptions (Speculative / Non-Canon by 
Default) 
5.1 Alternate Timelines (New Game+ Only) 
If ever implemented, temporal traversal must obey strict rules: 
●​ It is not a return to the past 
●​ It is entry into an alternate present 
●​ Previously resolved anomalies remain closed 

 
●​ Unresolved anomalies persist 
This preserves consequence while allowing exploration. 
[Placeholder: feasibility analysis and implementation risks] 
 
5.2 Lore-Based Constraints 
Any temporal interaction must be justified through lore constraints, such as: 
●​ critical moment windows only 
●​ the Infinitree remembering events, not individuals 
●​ entropy costs that destabilize the world 
[Placeholder: decide which constraints, if any, are canon] 
 
6. Forbidden Mechanics 
The following are explicitly disallowed: 
●​ freeform time travel 
●​ save/load abuse framed as narrative control 
●​ branching timeline explosion 
●​ explicit morality meters tied to time 
●​ "fix the past" mechanics 
Any feature violating these rules must be rejected. 
 
7. Design Enforcement 
This document takes precedence over: 
●​ individual mechanics proposals 
●​ feature requests 
●​ convenience-driven design decisions 
If a mechanic conflicts with the Temporal Design Law, the mechanic is invalid. 
 
8. Living Document Notes 

 
This is a stub intended for refinement. 
Sections marked as placeholders must be revisited before: 
●​ implementing new narrative systems 
●​ expanding Time Rift mechanics 
●​ designing New Game+ or post-game content 
All future time-related ideas should be validated against this document.

AI Use and Collaboration 
Artificial intelligence serves as a tool to support creative and technical workflows, not to 
replace human design. AI assistance is approved for concept generation, structural 
organization, and code prototyping, under the following directives: 
AI Good

Open questions / conflicts

Open: hierarchy between time rifts and anomalies not locked; naming conventions (full names vs given names) not fully locked. Time travel remains non-player-controlled; chat canon overrides docs.
